[^004-note41]: **marginal.** [194]

[^004-note42]: Taylor, *Original Sin*, pp. 126-27: "That is to say, '*Every Man is tempted [effectually*, is overcome by Temptation] *when he is drawn away by his*OWN *Lust*, when he suffereth himself to be governed by his OWN irregular Desires, *and enticed*into vicious Practices. *Then when Lust hath conceived*, is come to a full Purpose, Power and Maturity in the Heart, *it brings forth Sin*; *and Sin, when it is finished, bringeth forth Death*, or Destruction.'"

[^004-note43]: Taylor, *Original Sin*, p. 137, he considers David's words, that he was "shapen in inquity," and charges the Psalmist with throwing off his sin onto God. Pp. 187-89: "This Scheme [of original sin] was contrived to salve the Absurdity of God's being the Author of the Pollution of our Nature. But it will not do. For granting that God createth the Soul spotless, as no doubt he must; who infuseth it into the Body? God. And if it is polluted by being infused into the Body, who is the Author and Cause of its Pollution? God." In pp. 256-60, Taylor concludes his arguments: "Is it not highly injurious to the God of our Nature, whose Hands have fashioned and formed us, to believe our Nature is originally corrupted, and in the worst Sense of Corruption too?" (256). *Supplement*, p. 142 [419]: "But if Divine will say, that such Passions, Appetites, Propensities are *sinful*, and render us the Objects of God's Wrath and Displeasure; they should consider, 'tis infinitely absurd, and highly dishonourable to God to suppose he is displeased at us, and regards us as the Objects of his Wrath for what he himself has infused into our Nature."

[^004-note52]: Taylor, *Original Sin*, *Supplement,* p. 137 [413]: "Again; to say that God creates the Substance of our Nature, but the depraved and sinful Qualities, the *vicious Ferments*, which are in it are communicated to us from *Adam.* This also is absurd. For those Qualities must be communicated by some Power, or virtue continued from *Adam* to this Day. And if so, then it must be a Power superiour to God's Power; for while he is making his Work, this Power or Virtue communicates such Qualities to it as God hates, and doth not like should be in it: (for if God loves and likes our Sinfulness, then he can't be displeas'd at it, or it is all one as if he should himself immediately cause or Sinfulness.) Therefore this Power or Virtue is superior to the Power of God, for it produces Effects in his Work while he is making it, which he dislikes and abhors."

[^004-note54]: Taylor, *Original Sin,* p. 146: "For however the Will of Man may be concerned in *Natural Generation*, 'tis certain we produce one another just as much as the Oak produces the Acorn." [^004-note61]: Taylor, *Original Sin*, *Supplement*, p. 136 [413]: "To say he makes us according to his own *Original Decree*, or *Law of Propagation*, which obliges him to make us in a Manner which he abhors, is really making bad worse: For it is supposing him to be so defective in Wisdom, as, by his own Decree or Law, to lay such a Constraint upon his own Actions that he cannot do what he would, but is continually doing what he would not, what he hates to do, and what he condemns in us, *viz*. making us sinful, when he condemns us for making ourselves sinful."

may see them in p. 13, 150, 151, 156, 261, 384, 387.Taylor, *Original Sin*, pp. 13: "Here observe, that for any thing that appears in the Text, their [Adam's and Eve's] Sin, the evil Action they committed, was personal. . . . And as the evil Action they committed was personal, done only by them; so also must the real Guilt be personal, and belong only to themselves." Pp. 150-51: "How Mankind, who were perfectly innocent of *Adam*'s Sin, could for that Sin, and upon no other Account, be *justly* brought under God's Displeasure and Curse, we cannot understand, and by our Faculties must necessarily judge, according to all Rules of Equity, it is *unjust*. And therefore, unless our Understanding, or Perception of Truth, be false, that is, unless we do not understand what we do understand; or understand that to be true, which other Minds understand to be false, *it must be unjust*." P. 156, on the proposition that the Fall brought on humankind God's displeasure and curse: "By *Adam*'s one Sin and Transgression, and for no other Reason, all Mankind *are justly liable to all Punishments in this World*." P. 261: "Such Doctrines set Religion in direct Opposition to Reason and common Sense, and so render our rational Powers quite useless to us, and consequently Religion too. For a Religion which we cannot understand, or which is not the Object of a rational Belief, is no Religion for reasonable Beings." P. 384: "But surely had this [i.e. Adam being our federal head] too been a *fundamental Doctrine*, or even a Doctrine of Christianity, it would have been affirmed directly, clearly, and positively, and we should not have been left to grope for it in the Darkness of Conjecture and Supposition, which any Man may make at Pleasure, and every Man is at Liberty to reject." P. 38, on the parable of the talent: "This is Demonstration, that *Adam* was no *Representative* of moral Action to us; that we are all upon Trial as well as he; and that our Happiness or Misery is determined not by *his* Conduct but *our* own." absolutely impossible." see also p. 415. e 416. a.Taylor, *Original Sin,* *Supplement*, pp. 139-40 [415-16]: "Thus it stands clear to the meanest Capacity, and beyond all Controversy, that God (not the COURSE OF NATURE, which, consider'd apart from the Agency of God, is nothing, or no Cause at all; but God the Original of all Being, and the only Cause of all natural Effects) makes, forms, fashions, creates all Men, and every individual Man . . . No Substance can be made without some Qualities and Properties belonging to it. And it must necessarily, so soon as it is made, have those Qualities which the Maker gives it, and no other: For should it, in the very Instant it is made, and as it comes out of his Hands, have any other, then it would at the same time both *be* and *not be* what he has made it." It may not be improper here as a sort of Ap to subjoin something

as a Kind of Appendix to this Chapter, concerning The opinions of two

divines of no inconsiderable Note among the dissenters concerning a Partial

Imputation of Adams first sin One of them supposes that this sin is tho

n truly im truly imputed to Infants so [—] that thereby they are exposed

to a proper Punishmt yet is by not imputed to that degree as to Adam Himself

but expose to eternal Punishmt as it did Adam him but only to temporal

death or Annihilation On which I would observe That to suppose

that Imputes not all the Guilt of Adams sin to his Posterity but only a

very little Part of it relieves nothing but ones Imagination It is easier

to the Imagination to think of a poor little Infants bearing such Tor-

ments as they sometimes do in this ¤ & those Torments ending in

death & annihilation than to conceive of their suffering eternal misery

But it does not at all relive ones Reason . There is no Rule of Reason

that can be supposed to lie against the imputing sin the sin committed

by one to another that did not personally commit it it in the whole

but what will also lie against its being so imputed & punished in Part

For all the Reasons (if there are any) lie against the Imputation not the

Quality of what is imputed . They all lie against the transfer-

ring of Guilt from one to another a Person that committed a Fact to ano- [211]

ther who did not commit it The Thing that is unreasonable if there

be any thing unreasonable in the affair is this viz against the transfer-

ring of Guilt [ ] ring a Communication of Guilt from Person to Person

& not against the degree of what is derived derived If there be any

Rule of Reason that is strong & good that lies against a proper Impu

derivation or Communication of Guilt from one that acted to another that

did not act then it lies against all of that nature The Force of all

the Reasons (if the brought against Imputing Adams sin to his Posterity (if there

be any Force in em lie in this that Adam & his Posterity bent the same

But this lies as properly against imputing a Part Charging a Part of the

Guilt as the whole For Adams Posterity by not being the same with

Him had no more hand in a little of what was done than in a great deal

no more concerned in a Part than the whole & were as absolutely free

from being actually concerned in that act Partly as they were wholly And

there is Reason can be brought why one mans sin cant be justly

recked to anothers account wholly in the whole of it who was not then

in Being. wholly in the whole of it but what will properly lie

against its being recked to him in any Part that he should be subject to

any Condemnation or Punishmt on that Account . If those Reasons are

good all the difference there can be is this That to bring a very great

Punishmt on an Infant for Adams sin is a great Act of Injustice & to bring

a comparatively small Punishmt is a smaller Act of Injustice But not

that it is not as truly & demonstrably an act of Injustice as the other

To illustrate this by something parallel. Tis used as an Argument

why I maynt stand or take & use what is my neighbours as if it were

my own that He & I are not the same & therefore their Interests &

Properties are distinct now if this Argument be good it lies as truly

against my taking demanding & forcing from him a Part of the debt as

the whole of it indeed it is a greater Act of Injustice for me to take

from him the whole of it than a Part but no more truly & properly

an Act of Injustice . In order to determine whether there be Injustice

truly Injustice done the Question will not concerning the Qu Proportion

How much of is taken of what his neighbour owed but whether he

owed any thing on his Neighbours account or whether he is Justly

Chargeable with his Neighbours debts.

The other divine thinks there is truly an Imputation of Adams

sin Adams sin so that Infants cant be look'd upon as innocent

Creatures but yet Thinks it not agreeable to the Perfections of G.

to make the state of Infants in another ¤ worse than none a state

of Non-Existence on the Account of it . which is truly giving up the

whole Point of Imputation both of Adams sin both in whole & in Part

For it supposes it not to be right for God to bring any Evil on the Child of

Adam that is innocent as to personal sin without paying for it or balancing

it with Good which plainly supposes that the Child is not exposed to any

proper Punishment at all [-]on account of that Sin is not at all in debt to

divine Justice on the Account of Adams sin For if it were in debt then

surely Justice might take something from it without Paying for it If it

owes the suffering some Punishment then there is no need that Justice

should requite the Jus him or make the Infant for suffering that Punishmt

or make up for it by something that shall countervail it & in effect remove

& disannul it so that on the whole Good & Evil shall be at least an an even

Balance yea so that the scale of Good shall Preponderate If it be unjust

in a Judge to order any money to be taken from another without paying

him again & fully making it up to him it must be because he had Justly

forfeited Nothing at all

There are some Things in Dr T—rs Book besides Arguments that

are very likely to have Influence on the minds of unwary Readers &

young Students in divinity to prejudice them in Favour of the Author

and his Tenets One thing is the fair Pretext he there in many Pla-

ces exhibits of sincerity, modesty Humility Meekness & Charity in

his searching after Truth and proposing what he has found to others

his Thoughts & to others & laying the Reasons of them to others

And another thing of a very different in the same Book tho' of a

very different nature Kind yet is nature yet is of that sort that often

has much the same Influence on the minds of some Readers with

strong Arguments that is an assuming Boldness Positiveness

& Confidence of expression a magisterial Positiveness with airs

of Contempt of Opinions & Arguments of those whom they oppose

such things by some Readers pass for signs of a strong Genius

& masterly Abilities Argument of a Consciousness of a vast superiour

superiority to their Antagonists They judge of superiority in a Pole-

mic writing in a Literary Controversy something in the same man-

ner as they would in a Boxing match He that she manifests

the boldest [-] stoutest spirit they in words & action They look

upon as superiour

But that neither of these Things in our Author neither his

specious Professions of sincerity Love & humble Opinion of Him-

self nor his supercilious dictatorial Peremptoriness may have

any other than their proper Influence One means to be used &

I think a very just one would be to compare these things together

& to observe how they agree To which End it may not be im-

proper to put them to some expressions of Each sort together one

against the other that both may be viewed at once together
